a:T47c,The approach is infrastructure-level rather than algorithmic. Anthropic says it has agreed to use all compute capacity at SpaceX's Colossus 1 data center, adding more than 300MW of capacity and over 220,000 NVIDIA GPUs within the month. This capacity is tied directly to higher Claude Pro and Claude Max capacity, doubled five-hour Claude Code rate limits for Pro, Max, Team, and seat-based Enterprise plans, removal of peak-hour reductions for Claude Code on Pro and Max, and higher API rate limits for Claude Opus models. The longer-term plan includes an Amazon agreement of up to 5GW, with nearly 1GW of new capacity by the end of 2026; a 5GW Google and Broadcom agreement starting in 2027; a Microsoft and NVIDIA partnership including $30B of Azure capacity; and a $50B US AI infrastructure investment with Fluidstack. A notable technical implication is heterogeneous hardware operation. Training and serving Claude across Trainium, TPUs, and NVIDIA GPUs can reduce supplier dependence, but it raises hard engineering problems in compiler stacks, collective communication, numerical behavior, parallelism strategies, and deployment portability.b:T65c,SATFormer keeps the same high-level idea as prior value-residual architectures: later layers can reuse the first-layer value projection V1. The change is in how access is controlled. ResFormer uses a layer-wise scalar λ_n, so every token and every head in a layer receives the same amount of V1. SATFormer replaces that with an input-dependent gate α_t,j^(n), computed from the normalized hidden state x_t^(n) at layer n and position t through a single linear projection W_alpha^(n) that outputs one gate per KV head. A ReLU is applied so the gate is non-negative and can become exactly zero. The resulting gate modulates, for each token and each KV head, how much of V1 is mixed into the current layer’s value computation. The design is intentionally minimal. The gate is token-wise and head-wise because token dependence alone would ignore head specialization, while head dependence alone would force uniform access across the sequence. The controller is just a linear projection, not an extra MLP or routing module, to keep parameter and runtime overhead negligible. And the architecture uses one early pathway rather than dense layer aggregation, avoiding the memory and bandwidth costs of DenseFormer-style designs. The paper’s main claim is not that SATFormer has much larger representational capacity, but that selective access is the right granularity for exploiting early features. The mechanistic analysis supports this: gate activations are sparse, depth-dependent, head-specific, and token-specific, suggesting that V1 is used as a structured retrieval pathway rather than a permanently open residual shortcut.c:T502,The empirical study spans models from 130M to 1.3B parameters. The main result is consistent improvement over both vanilla Transformers and ResFormer in validation loss across all tested scales, with stable gains in zero-shot evaluation. The headline numbers reported in the paper include an average zero-shot accuracy gain of about +0.67 over ResFormer at 1.3B, and roughly +1.5 points over ResFormer on retrieval-intensive evaluations. Against heavier dense alternatives such as MUDDFormer, SATFormer matches or exceeds performance while running at about 1.8x the throughput. That efficiency point matters: many cross-layer reuse methods look attractive in isolation but become less compelling once memory traffic and throughput are accounted for. The ablations are also important. One set shows that the V1 pathway itself is functionally necessary; removing it hurts language modeling. Another set replaces the input-dependent gate with coarser or more static controls, showing that the gain is not just from extra parameters but from conditional token/head-level access. The analysis section further examines gate sparsity, head concentration, depth patterns, and logit-lens-style evidence, arguing that the learned access pattern is structured and causally tied to performance.d:T484,Its relevance is not in pretraining methodology but in turning a representation-level property—low intrinsic dimensionality of activations—into a concrete inference design. That is more interesting than static weight compression because it is conditioned on the actual runtime input distribution. If the assumption continues to hold for larger models and longer contexts, it suggests that the effective parameter count at inference can be much smaller than the nominal parameter count, and that this effective count is input-dependent. For pretraining researchers, this raises two useful questions. First, can training explicitly encourage more stable and lower-dimensional layer representations, making downstream dynamic low-rank inference easier? Second, should model capacity be discussed not only in terms of total parameters, but also in terms of activation subspace dimension under realistic workloads? In contribution type, this is a solid inference-side systems/method paper rather than a training-paradigm shift, but the connection it draws between activation geometry, dynamic gating, and roofline bandwidth limits is technically meaningful.e:T901,The method, Gated Subspace Inference (GSI), has three main components. First, for each layer it maintains an online orthonormal basis V_k^(l) that approximates the k-dimensional subspace occupied by current token activations. The paper inherits the DGKS rank-1 update scheme from SSI, updating the basis along the time dimension during decoding rather than estimating a fixed offline PCA-like subspace. This matters because activation distributions drift with context, so a static basis can mismatch the actual sequence.

Second, the same layer-wise basis is shared across all linear maps in that layer: Q/K/V projections, attention output projection, and MLP up/down projections. For a linear map y = Wx, the input is decomposed as x = V_k g + r, where g = V_k^T x and r is the residual. The method precomputes and caches the low-rank weight image M = W V_k. On the fast path, it uses y ≈ M g, which requires reading M instead of the full W, reducing bandwidth from d_out × d to d_out × k. Compared with SSI, which only covered the two MLP projections, GSI extends the mechanism to the full transformer and therefore attacks a much larger fraction of linear-layer traffic.

Third, a per-token gate decides whether the residual correction is needed. The gate is based on the relative residual norm ρ_t = ||r_t|| / ||x_t||. If ρ_t is small, the activation lies mostly inside the tracked subspace and the fast path is used. If ρ_t exceeds a threshold, the method falls back to the slow path and computes the exact output, or at least the missing residual contribution Wr. The gate is not presented as heuristic sparsity; it is meant to control output-distribution error within a user-chosen tolerance. In other words, the method exposes a tunable trade-off between approximation and fidelity.

Relative to prior work, the differences are clear. It does not use a static low-rank factorization of weights; it performs activation-conditioned low-rank inference. It does not rely on neuron-wise or element-wise sparsity; it compresses along a continuous subspace. And it does not apply approximation unconditionally; it adds a residual-aware gate so difficult tokens can revert to exact computation. That makes the method more robust for language-model decoding, where token difficulty is highly uneven.f:T765,The evaluation covers three model families—GPT-2 124M, GPT-J 6B, and OPT 6.7B—on AMD MI300X. The headline result in the abstract is an effective 3.0× to 10.5× reduction in linear-layer weight reads, while keeping the perplexity ratio below 1.00 and preserving top-k behavior. The wording matters: the gain is reported for linear-layer weight-read traffic, not necessarily for end-to-end wall-clock throughput at the same multiplier, since attention, kernel overhead, cache maintenance, and gating also contribute to latency.

From the full text structure, the key empirical questions are likely organized around three trade-offs. First, the subspace dimension k versus quality and speed. Smaller k reduces the cached image size M = W V_k and saves more bandwidth, but increases the residual and therefore the fallback rate. The real gain depends on the balance between low-rank savings and slow-path frequency. Second, the gate threshold sensitivity. A looser threshold increases fast-path coverage but also increases approximation error; a tighter threshold approaches lossless inference but reduces acceleration. Third, the benefit of extending from SSI’s MLP-only scope to the full transformer. Since QKV and output projections account for roughly half of linear-layer traffic, including them materially increases the reachable savings.

The abstract does not provide finer-grained numbers such as per-model perplexity, exact top-k preservation, or ablations across k. When reading the tables, two checks matter most: whether quality remains stable on long contexts and harder prompts, and whether the largest speedups, such as 10.5×, occur only under aggressive k or permissive gating settings. For this class of methods, tail behavior matters more than averages, because a small fraction of high-residual tokens can trigger frequent fallback and erase practical throughput gains.10:T455,For a quick pass, start with the introduction’s roofline argument and the bandwidth diagnosis. Then read the method section around the decomposition x = V_k g + r, the cached image M = W V_k, and the residual gate. Those pages determine whether the method is conceptually sound. After that, focus on three kinds of figures and tables: layer-wise effective-rank or explained-variance statistics to validate the low-dimensionality assumption; the relationship among residual norm, gate trigger rate, and perplexity to understand when approximation is safe; and latency/bandwidth breakdowns to see where the measured gains actually come from.

A reasonable skim takes 20–30 minutes. If time is limited, read the overview figure, the basis-update algorithm, and the main results table. If deciding whether the idea is worth deeper follow-up, two questions deserve extra attention: whether sharing one layer-wise basis across QKV, O, and MLP is too coarse, and whether the online basis maintenance remains numerically stable and worthwhile for larger models, longer contexts, or speculative decoding pipelines.11:T58a,TC-JEPA keeps the basic I-JEPA teacher-student structure. An online encoder processes visible context patches, an EMA target encoder provides target features for masked patches, and a predictor maps context plus mask tokens to predicted target representations.

The key change is in the predictor. Instead of compressing the caption into a single global text embedding, the model introduces a fine-grained text conditioner. At multiple predictor layers, target patch prediction is modulated through sparse cross-attention over text tokens. This lets each predicted patch selectively attend to relevant words and implicitly learn patch-word correspondences.

The paper also adds regularization on patch-word similarities, including sparsity and consistency constraints. The purpose is to prevent diffuse or unstable alignments across layers, so that text genuinely reduces uncertainty rather than becoming an extra noisy channel.

This differs from CLIP-style contrastive learning in a fundamental way. CLIP aligns whole-image and whole-text representations and often sacrifices local detail for semantic invariance. TC-JEPA keeps feature prediction as the training objective, so it has a better chance of preserving dense visual information while still injecting semantics. It also differs from prior patch-text correspondence work because the correspondence is learned to support prediction, not contrastive matching.12:T494,The paper reports that TC-JEPA improves over I-JEPA and several language-supervised contrastive baselines on classification, dense prediction, and multimodal tasks, while also being more stable and scalable during training. The provided excerpt does not include the full numeric tables, but the qualitative pattern is already informative.

Two claims matter most. First, dense prediction improves, which suggests that text conditioning does not simply push the representation toward coarse global semantics. Second, training stability and scaling behavior improve relative to pure JEPA, which is consistent with the paper’s central hypothesis that text reduces target uncertainty.

The most relevant experimental checks are likely the ablations on the text conditioner design, the effect of sparsity and consistency regularization, comparisons against CLIP-like methods on segmentation/captioning/VQA, and scaling curves across model sizes. If those results hold, the key takeaway is not a single benchmark number but that JEPA becomes easier to optimize and more semantically grounded when conditioned on text, without clearly giving up local representational fidelity.13:T83d,The paper first formalizes a unified framework for GTokenLLMs with four stages: Graph Input (GI), Graph Embedding (GE), Graph Token (GT), and Text Output (TO). A text-attributed graph is encoded by a TAG encoder, then projected into the LLM input space as graph tokens, which are concatenated with natural-language instructions for downstream generation. This decomposition is useful because it localizes where failures may arise: graph encoding, projection/alignment, instruction understanding, or their interaction.

On top of that, the authors propose GTEval, an evaluation pipeline designed to probe graph-token understanding through instruction transformations rather than through new downstream tasks. The key idea is to vary instructions along two axes. Format-level transformations test whether the model is overly sensitive to superficial prompt rephrasing, template changes, or instruction organization. Content-level transformations test whether the model can actually adjust its use of graph tokens when the semantic content of the instruction changes, rather than relying on a fixed prompt-task association. The framework is applied to six representative GTokenLLMs.

The paper also includes internal analyses of token usage. It examines whether graph tokens are attended to across layers and how their utilization patterns change across models and instruction variants. The conclusion is nuanced: graph tokens are not ignored, and they do preserve task-relevant textual and structural information, but the way LLMs use them is unstable and often fails to generalize properly under instruction variation.

The authors further study extra instruction tuning and compare against Graph-Textualizing LLMs (GTextLLMs). Additional instruction tuning improves performance on original and seen instructions, but it does not solve the underlying understanding problem. GTextLLMs remain weaker on graph tasks despite stronger native language grounding, which suggests that the issue is not just language understanding capacity, but the fragility of how graph representations are aligned into the LLM space.14:T680,The experiments cover six representative GTokenLLMs on text-attributed graph settings. The main findings are fourfold.

First, current GTokenLLMs do not fully understand graph tokens. Under format-level instruction changes, some models show large performance swings, indicating oversensitivity to prompt form. Under content-level changes, they can also be insufficiently responsive, indicating that they are not truly combining instruction semantics with graph-token semantics.

Second, these models rely heavily on textual attributes for reasoning, while their use of structural information is less robust. In other words, graph tokens may encode both text and topology, but the text component often dominates actual decision-making.

Third, attention and representation analyses show that graph tokens are not simply ignored. They are attended to across layers and retain task-relevant information. The problem is that their utilization patterns vary substantially across models and instruction variants, which points to weak and unstable semantic alignment.

Fourth, extra instruction tuning only partially helps. It improves performance on the original prompt and on seen instruction variants, but generalization to unseen transformations remains limited. That suggests the models are learning prompt adaptation more than genuine graph-token understanding.

The provided excerpt does not include the full result tables, so it would be unsafe to restate exact per-model numbers here. The important empirical message is the pattern: task effectiveness of graph-prefix tokens does not automatically imply robust semantic understanding under instruction variation.15:Tbed,方法的主线很清楚：先定义 anime 生成里什么叫“对”，再让模型逐步摆脱物理先验，最后建立新的优劣判别标准。

第一部分是 Production Knowledge System。作者认为 anime 数据自身过于多样，不能指望模型从原始像素里自动归纳出稳定的艺术先验，所以先人为提供一个结构化中介。这个系统至少包含两层：一层是工业化制作 taxonomy，把片段分解到 Style、Motion、Camera、VFX 四个可控轴；另一层是 AniCaption，把视频片段转成更像导演指令的语言，而不是普通视频 caption。这里的关键不是“多写一点描述”，而是把监督信号从结果描述改成生产变量描述。论文还提到用 graph-based multimodal reasoning 辅助标注，说明它不是纯模板抽取，而是试图从画面、时序和语义关系里推断制作意图。配合 dual-channel conditioning，模型一边接收精确的生产控制，一边接收更开放的艺术意图，从而避免单一文本条件既要承担硬约束又要承担模糊风格表达的冲突。

第二部分是 progressive style-motion-deformation curriculum。这是全文最像训练方法创新的地方。作者把 anime 相对自然视频的偏离拆成三条轴：风格多样性、运动幅度、形变强度。直接把高形变、高风格方差样本混进训练，会让已有物理 prior 与目标分布冲突过大，出现早期 collapse。于是他们采用渐进式课程，让模型先在更接近物理、风格更收敛、形变更弱的子分布上适应，再逐步扩大到更极端的表达。这个设计比常见的按分辨率或噪声级别 curriculum 更贴近 domain gap 的来源。它隐含的判断是：anime 迁移的难点不是单一复杂度，而是多个偏离维度叠加后导致优化面骤变，因此需要分轴控制训练路径。

第三部分是 deformation-aware preference optimization。作者指出，标准视频指标如 FVD、CLIP score 会把“艺术性变形”和“坏掉的结构”都视作偏离自然分布，因此无法作为 anime 域的可靠质量信号。为此他们引入领域奖励模型，并做偏好优化，让模型学会在“允许变形”的前提下仍保持角色一致性、动作意图、镜头节奏和特效合理性。这里的价值不只在于加了 reward model，而在于 reward 的判别边界被重新定义：不是惩罚一切 physics violation，而是惩罚没有艺术意图支撑的失真。若全文给出了偏好数据构造、正负样本来源、以及 reward 与生成器联训或后训的细节，这部分会很值得细看。

与已有方法相比，AniSora 这类工作更多是在 physics-trained video backbone 上加控制模块；AnimeReward 则偏向用人类反馈做质量对齐。AniMatrix 的区别在于，它把问题前移到预训练目标和数据组织层面：控制、课程学习、偏好优化三者围绕同一个“artistic correctness”定义协同，而不是在原有 realism objective 上做局部修补。16:T498,Its relevance to pretraining is broader than the anime setting. First, it makes a useful point about prior mismatch: a foundation video model trained under a realism objective may be systematically misaligned with domains whose structure is governed by production conventions rather than physical laws. That matters for stylized video, synthetic worlds, game footage, UI interaction video, and potentially multimodal generative models that mix natural and non-natural data. Second, the paper does not treat the issue as a simple fine-tuning problem. It proposes a full transition recipe: redefine supervision, bridge the prior gap with curriculum, then rebuild quality signals with domain-aware preference optimization. That is a meaningful template for domain adaptation under severe objective mismatch. Third, the move from descriptive captions to production-oriented directives is conceptually aligned with a larger trend in pretraining: replacing surface-level reconstruction targets with latent factor or process-level supervision. The contribution is less about a single algorithmic trick and more about a coherent training framework for a difficult target distribution.17:Tcef,The method is organized around a three-step transition: define artistic correctness, progressively override the inherited physics prior, and then distinguish valid artistic deformation from failure.

The first component is the Production Knowledge System. The authors argue that raw anime data is too diverse for a stable implicit prior to emerge on its own, so they introduce structured supervision. This includes an industrial production taxonomy that factorizes clips along four controllable axes: Style, Motion, Camera, and VFX. On top of that, they build AniCaption, which is not a standard captioner describing visible content, but a system that infers production variables and verbalizes them as creator- or director-like instructions. This is an important shift. The conditioning signal is no longer “what is in the clip,” but “how the clip should be authored.” The paper also mentions graph-based multimodal reasoning in the annotation pipeline, suggesting that the labels are inferred from relations across frames, semantics, and visual cues rather than extracted from a flat template. A dual-channel conditioning interface then separates precise production control from more flexible artistic intent, which likely reduces the burden on a single text channel to encode both hard constraints and soft style cues.

The second component is a progressive style-motion-deformation curriculum. This appears to be the main training innovation. The gap between natural-video priors and extreme anime expression is decomposed into three axes: style diversity, motion amplitude, and deformation intensity. Instead of mixing all anime samples at once, which the authors claim leads to early collapse, training proceeds through a controlled schedule that gradually increases difficulty along these axes. The rationale is strong: the optimization difficulty is not just “harder samples,” but a compounded mismatch across multiple dimensions that each break the original prior in a different way. A curriculum aligned with the source of domain shift is more principled than generic curricula based only on resolution or noise level.

The third component is deformation-aware preference optimization. Standard metrics such as FVD or CLIP-based similarity are poorly suited here because both intentional stylization and pathological corruption can look like deviations from natural video statistics. The paper therefore introduces a domain-specific reward model and uses preference optimization to teach the generator what counts as good anime deformation versus broken structure. The key idea is not merely adding a reward model, but redefining the reward boundary itself: physics violations are not uniformly bad; unsupported distortions are. If the full paper provides details on preference data construction, positive/negative pair generation, and how the reward model is integrated into training, that section is likely one of the most informative parts.

Compared with prior anime-generation work that mainly adds control modules or post-hoc alignment on top of physics-trained backbones, AniMatrix seems to intervene earlier, at the level of supervision design and optimization trajectory. Control, curriculum, and preference learning are all tied to the same target notion of artistic correctness.18:T8fa,The visible full-text excerpt does not include the complete experimental tables, so the strongest conclusions here are about evaluation design rather than all exact numbers. The paper is clearly set up to validate three claims.

First, it should compare against general-purpose video models or straightforward anime fine-tuning baselines. The expected result is that physics-biased models produce smoother but artistically flattened motion, while AniMatrix better preserves exaggerated action, impact framing, and style-specific expression. For this paper, subjective evaluation and domain-specific quality judgments matter more than generic video metrics alone.

Second, the curriculum should be tested through ablations. This is likely the most important experimental section. The paper needs to show that the three-axis curriculum specifically reduces early collapse, rather than any sample ordering giving similar gains. Useful evidence would include more stable training curves, better structural consistency as sample difficulty increases, and clear degradation when removing style, motion, or deformation progression. Direct mixed training should ideally show collapse modes or severe artifact rates.

Third, the reward model and preference optimization need to demonstrate that they can distinguish intentional exaggeration from failure better than generic metrics or judges. The most convincing evidence would be pairwise preference win rates, human evaluation, or stratified analysis across different deformation intensities. It is also important to check whether preference optimization improves quality without hurting controllability.

Given the emphasis on the Production Knowledge System, AniCaption, and dual-channel conditioning, the paper likely includes supervision ablations as well: descriptive captions versus production-oriented directives, single-channel versus dual-channel conditioning, and possibly the effect of graph-based multimodal reasoning in annotation. Those ablations are essential, because otherwise gains could be attributed simply to more annotation effort or more data.

Since the excerpt does not expose model size, training scale, or exact deltas, those details should be verified directly in the main tables and appendix before drawing strong conclusions.19:T828,Read it along three threads: problem formulation, optimization path, and evaluation criterion.

Start with the introduction and the overview figure. The key question is whether the paper convincingly argues that physical realism is the wrong target for anime generation. If that argument is weak, the rest may look like a heavily engineered domain-specific stack.

Then focus on the Production Knowledge System. Check whether the four production axes are well defined, reasonably disentangled, and broad enough to cover anime production choices. AniCaption deserves close attention: is it merely a richer captioner, or does it explicitly encode latent production factors such as camera intent, deformation style, timing, and VFX rhythm? Any annotation examples, taxonomy coverage statistics, or inter-annotator consistency analysis are worth reading carefully.

Next, spend time on the curriculum section and the associated training curves. This is probably the most valuable part for a pretraining researcher. Look for how difficulty is quantified, how the three axes are scheduled, why all three are needed, and what evidence is provided for improved stability. If the paper includes collapse case studies, loss/gradient behavior, or stage-transition heuristics, that is where much of the practical insight will be.

After that, read the reward model and preference optimization section. The main question is whether the reward signal truly reflects anime quality rather than natural-video realism in disguise. Comparisons against FVD, CLIP-based metrics, or generic VLM judges would be especially informative.

Finally, inspect the failure cases and limitations. Important questions include identity consistency under strong deformation, possible conflicts between the two conditioning channels, whether the taxonomy constrains open-ended generation, and how annotation-heavy the pipeline is. A full careful read is likely a 60–90 minute paper; for a targeted pass, prioritize the method overview, curriculum ablations, preference evaluation, and annotation examples in the appendix.1a:T8b4,The method is a three-stage Draft-Evaluate-Diffuse pipeline. The novelty is less about any single component and more about how the three conditioning signals complement each other.

Stage 1 is Draft. Given a prompt p, the LLM generates a coarse visual draft d in the form of discrete vision tokens. This draft is not treated as the final image. It is a low-fidelity scene plan. The paper argues that this step is necessary because, without a concrete visual instance, later evaluation would collapse into generic prompt rewriting rather than grounded diagnosis.

Stage 2 is Evaluate. The same LLM takes the original prompt and the draft, compares them, and outputs a grounded evaluation e: a concise textual description of what is wrong or missing in the draft. This is the key move in the paper. The system exploits the empirical asymmetry that the unified model is better at post hoc diagnosis than at one-shot generation. Instead of asking the model to satisfy all constraints directly, it first asks it to create something inspectable and then to critique that object.

Stage 3 is Diffuse. A frozen SANA diffusion model is conditioned jointly on (p, d, e). The prompt provides the global semantic target, the draft provides a spatial anchor and scene layout, and the evaluation provides localized corrective instructions. The paper repeatedly argues that these signals are complementary: draft alone would preserve its own mistakes, evaluation alone without a visual anchor would reduce to prompt rewriting, and prompt alone returns to the standard text-conditioned diffusion setup.

Relative to prior work, this is not just prompt enhancement, because the evaluation is grounded in a specific visual draft. It is also different from generic coarse-to-fine generation, because the refinement signal is explicit language feedback rather than an implicit latent refinement path. From a pretraining perspective, the method also encodes a practical division of labor: the LLM handles discrete planning and semantic verification, while diffusion handles high-fidelity rendering. That division is more realistic than expecting one model to jointly master fine-grained pixel synthesis and compositional reasoning at the same level.1b:T6e6,The main empirical result is clear: with the same frozen SANA diffusion backbone, UniReasoner improves prompt faithfulness rather than merely image quality. The headline numbers reported in the paper are 0.79 to 0.88 on GenEval, an absolute gain of 0.09, and 84.50 to 86.30 on DPG-Bench, a gain of 1.8 points. The larger signal on GenEval is especially important because that benchmark stresses compositional alignment.

The paper also provides qualitative evidence for the central hypothesis. Typical examples include counting failures where the model generates five apples for a prompt asking for four, yet correctly identifies the mismatch when asked to evaluate the generated image. Similar patterns appear for spatial relations and physical plausibility. These examples matter because they validate the claimed asymmetry directly, rather than relying only on benchmark aggregates.

The most important ablations are the conditioning variants: prompt only, prompt plus draft, prompt plus evaluation, and prompt plus both. The paper’s argument is that the full combination works best because draft and evaluation carry different information: visual anchoring versus explicit correction. Draft alone can lock the generator into the draft’s mistakes; evaluation alone lacks a concrete spatial reference. Even though the provided excerpt does not include every table entry, the logic of the ablation and the reported gains strongly suggest that the improvement comes from complementary conditioning rather than from simply adding more tokens.

Another important design choice is the frozen generator. Since SANA is not retrained, the gains can be attributed mainly to the reasoning and conditioning interface, which makes the result cleaner and more transferable.1c:T4fb,Start with the introduction and the motivating example around the understanding-generation gap. That example is the paper’s foundation. If the asymmetry feels real, the rest of the design becomes easy to follow.

Then read the method section with one question in mind: why is grounded evaluation necessary, instead of plain prompt rewriting or self-refinement text? The answer defines the paper’s real contribution. The draft is not there for quality; it is there to create an inspectable object. The evaluation is not there to paraphrase the prompt; it is there to produce actionable corrections tied to that object.

In the experiments, prioritize two things. First, the main benchmark tables on GenEval and DPG-Bench, especially where the gains concentrate. Second, the ablations and failure cases when draft or evaluation is removed. Those parts reveal the role of each conditioning signal.

For deeper study, three questions are worth tracking. How sensitive is the method to draft quality? How much does the wording and granularity of the evaluation affect diffusion guidance? And can the single-pass pipeline be extended into a multi-round draft-critique-refine loop? Those are the places where the idea could evolve into a broader training or inference framework.1d:T499,Uni-OPD uses a dual-perspective recipe. From the student side, it improves exploration with two balancing strategies. Offline difficulty-aware balancing shapes the training distribution by sample difficulty, so training is not dominated by trivial examples or by examples that are too hard to learn from. Online correctness-aware balancing then uses the student’s current rollout correctness to keep training focused on informative states, especially near the boundary between correct and incorrect behavior. From the teacher side, the paper introduces outcome-guided margin calibration. Vanilla OPD trusts the teacher’s token probabilities on student rollouts, but those rollouts can be out of distribution for the teacher. The proposed calibration uses outcome reward as a global anchor: after aggregating token-level teacher scores over a trajectory, correct trajectories should rank above incorrect ones by a margin. If this order consistency is violated, the teacher signal is adjusted. This is meant to handle teacher degradation on unusual student prefixes, fluent but wrong reasoning chains, conflicts between multiple teachers, and unstable multimodal supervision.1e:T543,The method decomposes evaluation into rubric generation and rubric-conditioned judging. A rubric is considered useful if it helps a judge assign a higher score to a preferred response than to a dispreferred one. Rubric quality is therefore directly trainable through discriminative utility, measured by the score gap induced by the rubric. The paper formulates rubrics as latent variables and derives a training objective from a variational-inference view. Training alternates between two phases. In the policy phase, a frozen rubric model generates rubrics for questions; the policy samples multiple responses; a frozen judge scores the responses under the rubric; GRPO updates the policy using these rubric-derived rewards. In the rubric phase, preference pairs are built through temporal contrast: current-policy responses are treated as preferred, earlier-checkpoint responses as dispreferred. The rubric generator samples candidate rubrics, and receives reward when the judge conditioned on that rubric separates the pair in the right direction with a larger gap. This differs from GPT-4.1-generated rubrics or labeled rubric training because it does not require an external rubric teacher. It also differs from scalar reward modeling because it learns an explicit, portable evaluation artifact rather than only a compressed preference score.1f:T49f,The experiments cover twelve benchmarks spanning math, code, general reasoning, knowledge, instruction following, and open-ended generation. On the OLMo3-Adapt suite, EvoLM reaches a 69.3% average, compared with 66.7% for GPT-4.1-prompted rubrics. A scalar reward model that performs strongly on held-out preference benchmarks, with 86.4% on RewardBench 2 and 80.8% on JudgeBench, produces the weakest downstream policy at 59.7%, trailing EvoLM by 9.6 points. This is a useful result: offline reward-model accuracy is not sufficient to predict RL usefulness. The paper also reports broader generalization. The co-evolution procedure extends to OLMo-3-7B. Learned rubrics transfer to unseen policies and judges without retraining. On out-of-distribution deep research tasks, EvoLM has higher pairwise agreement with expert human rubrics than GPT-4.1: 58.4% versus 52.5% on HealthBench, and 59.3% versus 51.0% on ResearchQA. Qualitative analysis suggests that the trained generator learns to break holistic evaluation into checkable subcriteria, sometimes embedding expected intermediate values directly into the rubric, which allows even a 1.7B-parameter judge to score more reliably.20:T83a,The method, Side-by-Side (SxS) Interleaved Generation, keeps the setup deliberately simple. It uses a standard autoregressive model, a single context, and the same hidden-state evolution. The only addition is lightweight tagging that distinguishes two action types inside one token stream: think tokens and speak tokens. Think tokens update the context but are not disclosed to the user. Speak tokens are both part of the context and an early-released prefix of the final answer. The key constraint is not merely to speak early, but to speak only when the disclosed prefix is supported by the reasoning generated so far.

Training has two stages. The first is entailment-aligned interleaved supervision. Starting from ordinary triples (x, r, a), where r is reasoning and a is the final answer, the paper aligns answer prefixes with reasoning prefixes so that each early disclosure is justified by the available reasoning prefix. This is the central mechanism that avoids two degenerate solutions: unsupported early commitments and low-information filler text that improves superficial latency metrics without helping the user.

The second stage is SFT followed by RL. SFT teaches the model the semantics of the dual-action format and the pacing structure. But the paper argues that SFT alone is insufficient because the interleaved format changes the rollout distribution: the model may learn the format while losing some reasoning quality under the new commitment constraints. RL is then used to recover task performance and optimize the trade-off between correctness and content latency. In other words, RL is not just a score booster here; it helps the model re-adapt its reasoning policy under the new disclosure regime.

Relative to prior work, SxS differs from template-based interleaving because disclosure timing is learned rather than fixed. It differs from dual-channel or latent-reasoning systems because there is no separate hidden channel or second decoder. It also differs from system-level latency optimization because the target is supported content timing, not just earlier token emission.21:T67f,The evaluation spans two Qwen3 architectures, one MoE and one dense, at two scales including 30B-A3B and 4B. The benchmarks are AIME25 for in-domain mathematical reasoning and GPQA-Diamond for out-of-domain scientific QA. Importantly, the paper does not evaluate only final accuracy. It also reports token-level content-latency proxies, such as when the first supported visible progress appears and how long users wait between updates. That matches the problem formulation much better than plain TTFT.

The main empirical claim is consistent across architectures, scales, and domains: SxS improves the accuracy-latency trade-off without changing the model architecture. At a similar final accuracy, the model can disclose useful supported progress earlier; at a similar content latency, it can achieve better final task performance. The evidence is presented as a Pareto improvement rather than a single scalar gain.

Two ablation-level findings matter most. First, entailment-aligned supervision is necessary. If the objective naively rewards earlier output, the model tends to produce filler or unstable early commitments. Second, RL is necessary. SFT alone teaches the interleaving format but hurts reasoning quality, which confirms that the new format induces a nontrivial distribution shift. RL restores reasoning performance while preserving better pacing behavior.

The provided full-text excerpt does not include the complete result tables, so it is not safe to restate exact percentage gains or absolute latency numbers here. The paper’s empirical weight lies more in the shape and consistency of the Pareto shift than in one benchmark headline number.22:T422,Multilingual Self-Distillation (MSD): teacher and student are the same base LLM but conditioned differently.
- Student sees only the target low-resource query x_tgt.
- Teacher additionally sees the aligned English query x* (from a multilingual parallel/jailbreak dataset) plus a CoT instruction C that elicits safe reasoning in English while requiring the final answer in the target language.
Training distills the teacher’s safe output distribution/trajectory into the student so that the student learns safer behavior given only x_tgt.
The framework is presented as compatible with both off-policy self-distillation (teacher-generated data) and on-policy variants (student sampling with teacher guidance).
Dual-Perspective Safety Weighting (DPSW) reweights the distillation loss at the token level using both teacher and student confidence/divergence signals. The intent is to upweight tokens that are safety-critical and where the student deviates from the teacher, while downweighting uninformative tokens, making distillation more targeted and robust.23:T46e,They implement a black-box, three-stage pipeline: Encode (transform the harmful prompt), Submit (attach a strategy-specific instruction and query the target model), Evaluate (use a benchmark-standard judge model to label success/refusal). The key methodological move is splitting encodings into:
- LLM-based encodings: a helper LLM performs a genuine semantic rewrite into a self-consistent math problem (Set Theory, newly proposed Formal Logic via first-order logic/proof framing, and Quantum Mechanics framing). Helper prompts use an academic persona plus a couple of few-shot examples to reduce helper refusals and stabilize the transformation.
- Rule-based encodings: deterministic, surface-level math formatting (e.g., addition-equation templates, conditional-probability templates, symbol injection) that largely preserves the original text.
For LLM-based attacks, the submission instruction is intentionally not “decode”, but “solve the math problem and provide practical real-world examples”, which forces the target model to map the formal solution back into concrete procedures—effectively undoing the obfuscation.24:T60b,The paper is a controlled reward-design study rather than a new RL method. It compares several reward choices under two critic-free policy-gradient algorithms, GRPO and RLOO, across multiple pretrained code-capable base models. The reward variants include standard binary reward, direct pass-rate reward, reweighted pass-rate variants, and two-stage schedules that combine dense and sparse rewards.

The analysis has two layers. First, the authors verify that pass-rate rewards are indeed denser: many rollouts receive intermediate scores instead of collapsing to all-zero feedback. So the failure mode is not the absence of signal.

Second, they study whether those intermediate rewards induce useful gradient directions. Their central claim is that pass rate is miscalibrated with respect to full correctness. A program can achieve a high pass rate through brittle heuristics, memorized patterns, or overfitting to the provided tests while still being far from correct. Conversely, a nearly correct solution with a small bug may receive only a modest pass rate. Under group-relative updates, this causes ranking errors inside the sampled batch. When no rollout fully solves the problem, different partial solutions push the policy in different directions, and the resulting update tends to be weak or cancel out.

This is the key distinction from naive reward shaping: the paper argues that numerical granularity is not enough. What matters is whether the surrogate reward preserves a meaningful optimization geometry toward the target behavior.25:T445,From the provided full-text content, the experimental setup spans multiple base models, two critic-free RL algorithms, and several reward designs. The headline result is consistent across these settings: pass-rate rewards do not reliably outperform binary rewards in final code-generation performance.

The most important empirical observations are about training dynamics rather than a single benchmark number:
- pass-rate rewards increase the fraction of non-zero-reward rollouts;
- this extra density does not translate into better pass-all-tests outcomes;
- on hard problems where no rollout is fully correct, partial-pass samples produce noisy and weak policy updates;
- some high-pass-rate programs are shortcut solutions rather than genuinely correct ones;
- reweighted or two-stage variants may help optimization in limited regimes, but they do not overturn the main conclusion.

The excerpt provided here does not include the full numeric tables, model sizes, or exact benchmark scores, so those should be checked directly in the main results tables and appendix rather than inferred.26:T8c0,DGPO 的核心思路是把“相对参考策略的分布偏离”从单纯的 regularization penalty，改造成 token 级 advantage 重分配的依据。方法仍然是 critic-free，不训练额外 value 网络，而是在 GRPO 的组采样框架上重写 token 权重。

第一部分是距离度量替换。论文认为 reverse KL 有两个问题：一是无界，偏离大时梯度可能很尖；二是偏 mode-seeking，会强烈压制低概率但可能有价值的新轨迹。DGPO 用 Hellinger distance 代替 reverse KL。Hellinger 是有界的，数值范围稳定，因而更适合把“与参考分布不同”解释成一种可控的探索强度，而不是必须压回去的错误。这里的关键不是简单换一个 f-divergence，而是换掉了训练信号的语义：偏离不再只意味着风险，也可能意味着关键推理跳跃。

第二部分是 token 级 credit reallocation。对一条获得正向回报的序列，DGPO 不再把同一个 advantage 平均施加到所有 token，而是根据每个 token 相对参考策略的分布偏离程度，动态分配更大的或更小的更新权重。直观上，若某个 token 在当前策略下与参考模型差异更大，它更可能对应一处真正推动解题成功的探索步骤，因此应获得更高 credit。这样做的目标，是把 sequence-level reward 拆成 process-like supervision，但又不需要人工过程标注或 value model。

第三部分是 entropy gating。仅靠偏离程度会奖励“假创新”：模型也可能对错误、幻觉、离谱 token 给出高置信度，从而产生大偏离。为此，论文引入基于策略熵的门控，用归一化 epistemic uncertainty 去缩放 Hellinger 信号。高偏离且高不确定性的 token 更像是在探索；高偏离但低熵、高置信的 token 更可能是自信地胡说，因此其 credit 会被压低。这个设计很关键，因为它把“探索”与“幻觉”做了一个粗分离。

从算法形态看，DGPO 仍保留 GRPO 的组相对优化框架和低内存特性，没有引入 critic，也没有走到完整 process reward model。它试图占据一个中间位置：比序列级 RL 更细，但比 token-level verifier 或 step-level RM 更轻。27:T413,Its contribution is not to pretraining directly, but it is relevant to the pretrain–posttrain interface. In long-horizon reasoning RL, the bottleneck is increasingly not whether a reward exists, but how that reward is propagated through the generated process. Better token-level credit assignment means the behavioral change induced by RL can be more localized, instead of simply increasing the probability of the entire chain of thought. That matters for understanding whether RL is actually shaping reasoning patterns rather than just response style. The second useful point is that reverse KL may be a poor default if the goal is to discover new reasoning trajectories. Reinterpreting policy deviation as a guidance signal rather than only a penalty is a sensible move, especially in verifiable domains such as math. In contribution type, this is an objective-design paper rather than a paradigm shift, but it targets a real pain point in current critic-free reasoning RL: balancing fine-grained credit assignment with stable exploration.28:T961,DGPO turns deviation from the reference policy into a token-level signal for redistributing credit. The framework remains critic-free: it does not train an auxiliary value network, and it stays close to the group-sampling setup of GRPO while changing how token updates are weighted.

The first change is the divergence measure. The paper argues that reverse KL is problematic because it is unbounded and strongly mode-seeking. Large deviations can create sharp gradients, and low-probability but potentially useful trajectories are heavily discouraged. DGPO replaces reverse KL with the Hellinger distance. Since Hellinger is bounded, it provides a numerically smoother notion of policy deviation and is better suited for treating deviation as controlled exploration rather than as an error that must always be suppressed. The important point is not merely swapping one f-divergence for another; it changes the meaning of the signal used during optimization.

The second change is token-level credit reallocation. For a positively rewarded sequence, DGPO does not broadcast the same advantage to all tokens. Instead, it redistributes the sequence-level advantage according to each token’s distributional deviation from the reference policy. Intuitively, tokens that differ more from the reference are more likely to correspond to the exploratory steps that made the solution succeed, so they receive larger updates. The goal is to approximate process-level supervision without requiring step annotations or a learned value model.

The third component is entropy gating. Distributional deviation alone can reward fake innovation: the model may confidently emit incorrect or hallucinatory tokens that are far from the reference policy. To reduce this failure mode, DGPO scales the Hellinger-based signal by normalized policy entropy, interpreted as epistemic uncertainty. High-deviation, high-uncertainty tokens are treated as plausible exploration; high-deviation, low-entropy tokens are more likely to be confident mistakes and are down-weighted. This is the key mechanism that tries to separate useful exploration from hallucination.

Algorithmically, DGPO stays in the middle ground. It is finer than sequence-level RL, but much lighter than full process reward modeling or token-level verification. That is why the paper emphasizes critic-free scalability rather than maximal supervision quality.29:T748,The evaluation focuses on AIME 2024 and AIME 2025, using 7B and 32B models. The headline numbers in the abstract are 60.0% Avg@32 on AIME 2024 and 46.0% on AIME 2025, outperforming the DAPO baseline among critic-free alignment methods. Another important result is efficiency: DGPO reportedly adds only about 3.6% time overhead relative to vanilla GRPO, with essentially the same memory footprint because no value network is introduced. That matters because the paper’s claim is not just better reasoning performance, but better credit assignment at nearly the same systems cost.

From the paper’s logic, the most informative experiments are likely not only the final benchmark scores. First, convergence comparisons against GRPO and DAPO matter: if token-level redistribution is genuinely more effective, the method should separate earlier during training, not only at the end. Second, the ablation replacing reverse KL with Hellinger should be read through the lens of stability—gradient behavior, policy drift, collapse frequency—not just task accuracy. Third, the entropy-gating ablation is central. If removing the gate hurts performance or increases hallucination-like behavior, that supports the claim that deviation alone is too noisy a proxy for useful reasoning steps. Fourth, consistency across 7B and 32B is important; if both scales benefit, the method is less likely to be a narrow sweet-spot trick.

The abstract does not provide many implementation details such as exact gains over DAPO, training steps, group size, rollout budget, or reward construction. Those details matter a lot for interpretation. In particular, the definition of Avg@32 and whether all methods use the same test-time sampling budget should be checked carefully, since evaluation protocol differences can materially change the apparent strength of reasoning RL results.2a:T466,For a skim, start with the introduction and method overview, and lock onto the two substitutions: uniform sequence-level credit becomes token-level redistribution, and reverse KL becomes bounded Hellinger distance. Then read the core equations to see exactly how token weights are computed from policy deviation and entropy gating. If the derivation is dense, read the pseudocode or training-flow figure immediately after; otherwise it is easy to remember the intuition but miss where the gradients actually go.

In the experiments, prioritize three items: the main results table, the ablation table, and the training-stability or convergence plots. The main table answers whether the method works, the ablations answer which component matters, and the curves answer whether it is actually more stable and faster to optimize. A practical skim takes about 25 minutes. If deciding whether the method is worth reproducing, focus on the exact definition of entropy gating, the implementation form of Hellinger distance, and whether the redistribution rule introduces length bias or systematically over-rewards high-entropy tokens.2b:T43f,The analysis uses controlled prompts of the form “X that is not Y is Z” and contrasts two hypotheses. Under suppression, the model attends to Y and suppresses tokens related to Y. Under construction, it constructs a representation for “not Y” and uses that representation to promote valid answers. The paper first measures negation sensitivity through logit differences, showing that models internally respond to negation even when their final answer is wrong. It then identifies late-layer shortcut attention heads that promote positive answers on negated prompts. The proposed Attention Sinking intervention weakens these attention modules and is used as a causal test. For the constructive pathway, the authors combine path patching, LogitLens, and Sparse AutoEncoders. The resulting account is: attention moves the negation signal to the Y position; mid-layer attention moves a constructed “not Y” representation to the output position; the same modules also suppress Y, but less strongly; MLP features then amplify the negated representation before the final prediction.2c:T4fa,Methodologically, it is cross-scale + cross-framework circuit tracing with causal validation:
- Backbones: Qwen-3 0.6B/4B/8B/14B.
- Memory frameworks: mem0 (flat KV memories with embedding retrieval) and A-MEM (Zettelkasten-style network with LLM-driven write/manage prompts).
- Pipeline decomposition: treat Write, Manage, and Read as separate forward passes with stage-specific prompts; Manage compares each new fact against top-5 retrieved neighbors and decides add/update/delete/none; Read answers using top-5 retrieved facts. Retrieval itself is embedding-based (no LLM call).
- Circuit tracing: use Pre-trained Linear Transcoders (PLTs) to replace MLPs with sparse encoder–decoders that yield more monosemantic features, then build weighted causal graphs from input tokens → features → output logits under a locally linear causal analysis.
- Findings are framed as circuit-level structure: (1) control circuits emerge earlier than content circuits; (2) Write and Read share a late-layer grounding “hub” substrate, with prompting/framing recruiting a functional grounding direction; (3) direct amplification/steering of content circuits is fragile across the pipeline, so they pivot to using circuit separation for read-out diagnosis rather than intervention.2d:T574,The paper formalizes prompt steering as an activation intervention on the same generated suffix tokens. It introduces two notions of the prompt-induced intervention: (i) an accumulated effect up to layer l (Δ_PS_acc), and (ii) a layer-local effect (Δ_PS_loc) obtained by “neutralizing” the steering-prompt token activations while propagating the steered suffix, isolating per-layer net changes.
Empirically, the prompt-induced intervention is strongly non-uniform across token positions: a few positions receive large shifts while many are barely affected. This contradicts the common Const family (ActAdd/CAA/ITI/ReFT-R1-style) that applies the same scalar coefficient α everywhere.
Based on this, the paper proposes Prompt Steering Replacement (PSR): keep a rank-1 direction z_attr but replace the constant α with token-specific coefficients α_i predicted from the current activations (a small, interpretable gating/regression module). PSR is trained to imitate the activation changes and/or behavioral effects produced by successful prompt steering, effectively distilling an implicit prompt mechanism into an explicit intervention.
The authors also spell out when PSR can approximate prompting: when the prompt’s dominant effect at the chosen layer can be approximated by a single direction with position-dependent magnitude, rather than multi-directional or more complex dynamics.2e:T780,MechaRule has two main stages.

First, it performs task-local rule extraction. Given a dataset and a success metric, the method splits examples into success and failure cases. It then builds an interpretable feature space using an LLM-based agent that inspects contrastive examples and proposes candidate predicates, such as divisibility conditions or output-range conditions. These automatically proposed predicates are combined with a small set of manual seed features, and sparse if-then rules are extracted with RuleSHAP. The goal is not a universal global explanation, but a compact symbolic hypothesis for a specific behavior.

Second, it performs neuron localization through contrastive hierarchical ablation. Instead of exhaustive single-neuron probing, the method relies on two empirical hypotheses: sparse gating, meaning a behavior is often controlled by a small set of neurons, and regime-conditional dominance/overlap, meaning intervention effects can be monotone, saturating, and partially masked by dominant neurons. The paper calls this masking pattern overtopping.

Based on these assumptions, candidate neurons are organized into a hierarchy of nested groups. The method ablates groups first and only descends into branches whose group-level effect remains sufficiently large. The pruning criterion is not additive decomposition, but a high-confidence below-threshold group effect. This is important because intervention effects need not be linear or independent. The result is a more efficient search procedure that can still recover causally important neurons under overlap and saturation.

Finally, the extracted rules are anchored to the localized neurons. The rules predict when a neuron should matter; interventions test whether that neuron actually controls the behavior. That is the central novelty: symbolic rules are tied to causal internal units rather than treated as standalone surrogates.2f:T405,From the provided full-text excerpt, the experiments are designed to validate the pipeline rather than to maximize benchmark performance. The main empirical claims are:
- a small number of agonist neurons can strongly disrupt rule-related behavior when neutralized, supporting the sparse-gating hypothesis;
- group-level intervention effects show approximate monotonicity and saturation within a fixed regime, which justifies hierarchical group ablation;
- dominant neurons and broad antagonistic effects can mask weaker but behavior-specific neurons, making adaptive descent preferable to flat exhaustive search;
- the extracted symbolic rules can predict which examples are controlled by particular neurons, suggesting a nontrivial alignment between symbolic behavior descriptions and internal causal structure.

The excerpt does not include the full experimental section, exact model sizes, task inventory, localization accuracy, or compute savings, so those details should be checked directly in the main tables and appendix.30:T432,Three main components:
1) Execution Subagent interface: the main agent delegates terminal work to a subagent with only a Terminal tool, a turn budget, and a system prompt enforcing structured summaries. The design choice is to isolate verbose intermediate outputs rather than forcing the main agent to ingest them.
2) Terminus-4B: Qwen3-4B post-trained for this role. Stage-1 SFT on internal telemetry trajectories teaches command selection, error interpretation, and summary formatting.
3) RL with GRPO plus a subagent-centric rollout framework: rollouts are constructed so each candidate starts from the same problem state while minimizing reliance on a strong main agent (they even use vanilla Qwen3-4B as the main agent during rollouts). Rewards come from a rubric-based LLM judge that scores candidates against reference trajectories across multiple quality/failure dimensions (e.g., capturing the key error lines, avoiding vague or hallucinated summaries, not getting stuck in unproductive command loops), yielding denser training signals than success/failure rewards.31:T43f,The method is straightforward. The authors start from an existing thinking-oriented base model, Qwen3-30B-A3B-Thinking-2507, and apply a single SFT stage on a compact but deliberately hard dataset. The dataset is produced with three modifications to the trajectory synthesis pipeline. First, they enlarge the source graph used for task generation, so each synthetic query is conditioned on a richer evidence subgraph with more possible reasoning paths and stronger multi-hop requirements. Second, they expand the tool set available to the ReAct-style agent, encouraging more diverse interaction patterns and broader functional coverage. Third, they apply strict low-step filtering: any trajectory solvable in fewer than a threshold number of tool calls is discarded. This raises the minimum difficulty floor and removes shallow lookup-style examples. The final training set contains only around 10k high-difficulty trajectories. The key design choice is to spend the complexity budget on the supervision distribution rather than on RL, memory modules, or more elaborate agent frameworks.3:["$","div",null,{"className":"mx-auto max-w-4xl px-4 py-8 sm:px-6 xl:max-w-6xl","children":["$","$L9",null,{"data":{"date":"2026-05-07","generated_at":"2026-05-07T00:36:51.106558+00:00","stats":{"total":99,"starred":8,"must_read":10,"skim":34,"glance":55,"by_category":{"industry":3,"llm_pretrain":5,"efficient":8,"multimodal":14,"multimodal_gen":8,"post_train":17,"mechanism":25,"coding_agent":4,"agent":15},"trending_repos":9,"fetch_status":{"arxiv":"ok","huggingface":"failed","blog":"ok","twitter":"empty","github_trending":"ok","hf_trending":"empty"},"fetch_errors":{"huggingface":"exhausted 3 retries + verify=False fallback; The cheapest among these methods add static value residuals: learned mixing coefficients that expose the first-layer value projection V_1 uniformly across tokens and heads. More expressive dense or dynamic alternatives recover finer-grained ","starred":false,"priority":"p2","reading_tier":"skim","source":"arxiv","sources":["arxiv"],"published_at":"2026-05-05T16:38:29+00:00","category_id":"llm_pretrain","cover_url":"/covers/2605_03953.png","hf_upvotes":0,"arxiv_categories":["cs.LG","cs.CL"],"summary":{"core_problem":"这篇工作处理的是一个很具体但又常见的 Transformer 深层信息流问题：早期层提取到的词法、局部语义和 token 级细粒度特征，在经过多层残差流、注意力和 MLP 反复变换后，后层未必还能稳定恢复。已有做法要么像 ResFormer 那样，用每层一个静态标量把第一层 value 投影 V1 注入后续层，代价低但控制过粗；要么像 DenseFormer、MUDDFormer、MUDD 类方法那样做更密集的跨层连接，表达力更强但显著增加显存和吞吐开销。论文要解决的核心问题是：能否只保留一条很便宜的早期表示通路，但让模型按 token、按 head、按上下文动态决定何时使用这条通路，从而在性能和效率之间拿到更好的折中。","importance":"对预训练研究的意义主要有两层。第一，它不是在注意力公式上做大改，而是在信息保真和跨层访问控制上做最小改动，属于那类容易并入现有基座训练栈的架构小改良。如果结论稳，这类方法比复杂跨层聚合更有落地性。第二，它把“早期表示复用”从连接设计问题改写成控制问题：不是问要不要加更多跨层边，而是问单条早期通路该被哪些 token、哪些 head、哪些层访问。这种 framing 对后续预训练架构研究有启发，因为很多性能-效率问题并不一定需要更密的结构，可能只需要更细粒度的条件化访问。贡献量级上，它更像一个扎实的架构增量，而不是范式切换；但如果目标是低成本提升中等规模 Transformer 的训练效率和检索能力，这个方向是有实际价值的。","technical_approach":"方法很克制。SATFormer 保留“把第一层 value 表示 V1 暴露给后续层”这一主线，但把 ResFormer 的层级静态系数 λ_n 换成输入相关的门控 α_t,j^(n)。具体做法是：对第 n 层、位置 t 的归一化隐藏状态 x_t^(n)，通过一个线性投影 W_alpha^(n) 直接产生每个 KV head 的门值；再经过 ReLU，得到非负且可为 0 的 gate。随后，该 gate 逐 token、逐 KV head 地调节当前层 value 与第一层 V1 的混合比例。论文强调了三个设计选择。其一，门控粒度是 token × head，而不是仅 token 或仅 layer。原因是不同 head 有功能分工，不同 token 对早期信息的依赖也不同。其二，gate 只用单层线性投影，不引入额外 MLP 或注意力控制器，目的是把参数和运行时开销压到接近 Transformer/ResFormer。其三，使用单条早期通路，而不是聚合多层历史，避免 Dense 类方法的内存和带宽成本。与已有方法相比，SATFormer 的区别不在于“接更多层”，而在于“更精细地决定接不接、接多少”。从全文分析看，作者想证明的不是更强的表示容量，而是更好的访问选择性。机制分析也围绕这一点展开：gate 在层间、head 间、token 间都呈现明显稀疏和结构化分布，说明模型没有把 V1 当成一个全局常开残差，而是在少数位置和少数头上有针对性地调用早期表示。","experiments":"实验覆盖 130M 到 1.3B 参数规模，主结论是 SATFormer 在所有评测尺度上都优于 vanilla Transformer 和 ResFormer 的验证损失，并在零样本任务上保持稳定收益。文中给出的代表性结果包括：在 1.3B 规模上，相比 ResFormer，平均零样本准确率提升约 0.67 个点；在 retrieval-intensive 评测上，相比 ResFormer 提升接近 1.5 个点；与更重的 dense alternatives 如 MUDDFormer 相比，效果可匹配或略优，但吞吐大约是其 1.8 倍。论文还强调 SATFormer 的吞吐和显存占用与 Transformer/ResFormer 接近，这一点对预训练很关键，因为很多跨层连接方法的收益会被系统代价吃掉。消融部分的价值较高：一类消融验证 V1 通路本身是必要的，去掉后性能回落；另一类消融把输入相关 gate 替换成更静态或更粗粒度的控制，结果说明收益不只是来自额外参数，而是来自 token/head 级条件化访问。机制实验还用了 gate 分布统计、head 稀疏性分析和 logit lens 式分析，显示早期表示访问集中在部分层和部分 head，且这种结构与语言建模性能相关，而不是训练噪声。","reading_guide":"泛读时建议先看 Introduction 里对 ResFormer、DenseFormer、MUDDFormer、HyperConnections 的定位，因为这决定了 SATFormer 的问题设定：它不是追求最强跨层聚合，而是追求最便宜的动态访问。然后重点看方法部分 gate 的定义和设计理由，尤其是为什么选择 per-token per-head、为什么只用线性投影、为什么用单条 V1 通路。实验部分优先看三类内容：一是不同参数规模下的 validation loss 和 zero-shot 主表，确认收益是否随规模稳定；二是 retrieval benchmark 的对比，理解它到底改善了什么能力；三是 ablation 和 gate analysis，这部分最能说明方法不是“多加一点参数所以更好”。如果时间有限，预计 20 到 30 分钟可以抓住主线；如果要判断是否值得复现，建议把附录里 gating mechanism 的替代设计和效率统计也看完。","authors_note":""},"summary_en":{"core_problem":"The paper addresses a specific depth-wise information flow issue in Transformers: lexical, structural, and token-level features extracted in early layers may become harder to recover after repeated transformations through attention, normalization, and MLP blocks. Existing fixes sit at two extremes. Static value residual methods such as ResFormer expose the first-layer value projection V1 to later layers with a single learned scalar per layer, which is cheap but too coarse. Denser cross-layer aggregation methods are more expressive, but they cost more memory and throughput. The central question here is whether a single early-value pathway can remain competitive if the model learns fine-grained control over when and where that pathway is used, instead of adding many more cross-layer connections.","importance":"For pretraining research, the contribution is less about a new modeling paradigm and more about a practical architectural refinement with a clean systems profile. The method changes very little in the Transformer stack, yet it targets a real failure mode: loss of recoverable early features with depth. More importantly, it reframes early-representation reuse as a control problem rather than a connectivity problem. Instead of asking how many skip pathways should be added, it asks which tokens, which heads, and which layers should access a single cheap early pathway. That framing is useful beyond this paper. In many pretraining settings, better conditional access can be more valuable than denser structure. The impact is moderate in scale, but meaningful if one cares about low-overhead improvements that can survive full pretraining constraints.","technical_approach":"$b","experiments":"$c","reading_guide":"Start with the introduction and related framing around ResFormer, DenseFormer, MUDDFormer, and HyperConnections. That section makes clear what SATFormer is trying to optimize for: not maximal cross-layer expressivity, but a better performance-efficiency trade-off from a single early pathway. In the method section, focus on the gate definition and the three design choices: token-by-head granularity, a single linear controller, and one V1 pathway instead of dense history aggregation. In the experiments, prioritize three parts: the scaling table for validation loss and zero-shot accuracy, the retrieval-heavy benchmark results, and the ablation plus gate-analysis sections. Those are the parts that tell you whether the method is genuinely selective rather than simply larger. For a quick pass, 20–30 minutes is enough to capture the main idea. A superior optimizer must satisfy three core desiderata: efficiency, achieving Muon-like preconditioning to accelerate optimization; stability, strictly adhering to the scale-invariance inherent in neural networks; and speed, minimizing computational overhead. While existing methods address these aspects to varying degrees, th","starred":false,"priority":"p2","reading_tier":"skim","source":"arxiv","sources":["arxiv"],"published_at":"2026-05-05T14:00:27+00:00","category_id":"llm_pretrain","cover_url":"/covers/2605_03769.png","hf_upvotes":0,"arxiv_categories":["cs.LG"],"summary":{"core_problem":"Nora 处理的是矩阵型优化器在 LLM 训练中的三方取舍：Muon 类正交化更新有较好的预条件效果，但 Newton-Schulz 迭代开销高；RMNP 类行归一化方法更快，但没有严格处理归一化网络中的尺度不变性，容易引入沿权重方向的径向扰动。论文希望得到一个同时满足预条件效率、尺度稳定性和低计算开销的矩阵优化器。","importance":"这篇工作对预训练研究的价值主要在优化器设计。它把两个常被分开处理的问题合在一起：一是 Muon 式矩阵预条件能提升数据效率，二是 LayerNorm、RMSNorm 等结构使很多权重矩阵的有效学习发生在角向空间，而不是权重范数方向。Nora 的意义不在于提出复杂的新二阶方法，而在于给出一个很便宜的近似：先去掉行级径向动量，再做行级归一化。这个设计适合大规模训练，因为它关注的是每步额外开销、学习率可迁移性和训练稳定性，而不是只看少量 benchmark 的最终 loss。","technical_approach":"方法的核心是 row-wise tangent update。给定权重矩阵 W 和动量 V，Nora 先把每一行动量分解为平行于对应权重行的分量和垂直分量，只保留垂直分量。这样更新方向在行级上与当前权重正交，减少不改变函数输出的径向噪声，也避免权重范数漂移导致有效学习率抖动。随后，Nora 对这个行级正交动量做归一化，用对角形式近似 Muon 的矩阵预条件。与 Muon 相比，它不需要 Newton-Schulz 迭代，避免了较高的矩阵计算开销；与 RMNP 相比，它不是直接归一化原始动量，而是先显式投影到尺度不变参数的切空间，因此更严格地满足 scale-invariance。论文还把 Nora 放到 μP 框架下分析，给出学习率随宽度缩放的条件，并提供非凸优化下的收敛保证。实际实现上，核心逻辑可以写成很短的矩阵代码，适合作为 Adam 或 momentum 类优化器的更新模块。","experiments":"实验以 LLaMA 架构训练为主，覆盖不同模型规模和一组学习率设置，比较 Nora、Muon、RMNP 以及常规优化器的收敛速度和 wall-clock 表现。关键结论是：Nora 在训练 loss 下降速度上接近或优于 Muon 类矩阵预条件方法，同时由于只做行级投影和归一化，单步开销更低；相对 RMNP，Nora 在较大学习率或更长训练中更稳定，论文将其归因于去除了径向动量噪声。消融实验的重点应是两部分：只做行归一化但不做正交投影，会保留范数方向扰动；只做正交投影但不做预条件，收敛加速不足。全文材料中没有给出可核验的具体参数量、token 数或提升百分比，因此不宜把实验量级解读得过重。","reading_guide":"建议泛读，约 30–45 分钟。优先看 Nora Optimizer 一节，重点是动量的行级正交投影公式和后续归一化步骤；再看 μP scaling 相关定理，判断它的学习率缩放假设是否适合自己的训练配置；最后看 LLaMA 训练曲线、学习率 sweep 和 wall-clock 对比。精力有限时，可以跳过较长的非凸收敛证明，只保留结论条件。值得深挖的是它对尺度不变性的处理：如果训练栈中大量使用 RMSNorm/LayerNorm，这类切空间更新可能比单纯追求更强预条件更稳。","authors_note":""},"summary_en":{"core_problem":"Nora addresses a practical trade-off in matrix-based optimizers for LLM training. Muon-style orthogonalized updates provide useful matrix preconditioning but rely on Newton-Schulz iterations and add noticeable compute overhead. RMNP-style row normalization is much cheaper, but it does not strictly respect the scale-invariant geometry induced by normalization layers, so radial momentum noise can perturb weight norms and the effective learning rate.","importance":"The main relevance is optimizer design for large-scale pretraining. The paper connects two issues that are often treated separately: matrix preconditioning for data efficiency, and tangent-space updates for scale-invariant parameters. Nora is useful because it proposes a cheap approximation rather than a heavier second-order method. It aims to preserve most of the benefit of Muon-like updates while controlling per-step overhead and reducing norm-direction jitter.","technical_approach":"The method uses row-wise tangent updates. For a weight matrix W and momentum V, Nora decomposes each momentum row into the component parallel to the corresponding weight row and the component orthogonal to it. It discards the parallel component and updates only with the row-wise orthogonal momentum. This removes radial motion that does not change the function for scale-invariant weights. Nora then applies a diagonal preconditioning estimate through row-wise normalization, which approximates the effect of a Muon-like matrix preconditioner without Newton-Schulz iterations. Compared with Muon, the method is much cheaper. Compared with RMNP, it first projects into the tangent space and therefore treats scale invariance more explicitly. The paper also analyzes learning-rate scaling under the μP framework and gives non-convex convergence guarantees.","experiments":"The experiments train LLaMA-style models at multiple scales and across learning-rate sweeps, comparing Nora with Muon, RMNP, and standard optimizers. The reported pattern is that Nora improves convergence speed while keeping wall-clock cost lower than heavier matrix optimizers. It is also more stable than plain row-wise momentum normalization, especially when learning-rate sensitivity and weight-norm drift matter. The most informative ablations are the separation between row-wise orthogonal projection and row-wise normalization: projection controls radial noise, while normalization provides the cheap preconditioning effect. The provided material does not expose reliable exact numbers for model sizes, token counts, or percentage gains, so the empirical magnitude should be interpreted with care.","reading_guide":"Skim in about 30–45 minutes. The method decomposes each activation vector into a subspace component and a residual, computes the linear-layer output on the subspace component via a cached low-rank weight image at reduced memory bandwidth, and applies a per-token gate that determines whether the residual correction is computed or skipped. The gate ensures that th","starred":false,"priority":"p2","reading_tier":"skim","source":"arxiv","sources":["arxiv"],"published_at":"2026-05-04T19:48:40+00:00","category_id":"efficient","cover_url":"/covers/2605_03109.png","hf_upvotes":0,"arxiv_categories":["cs.LG","cs.AI"],"summary":{"core_problem":"这篇工作处理的是单 batch、自回归解码阶段的一个老问题：Transformer 推理往往不是算力受限，而是显存带宽受限。每生成一个 token，都要反复从 HBM 读取大矩阵权重，线性层的读带宽成本近似是 O(Ld^2)。已有方法多从权重侧下手，比如量化、静态低秩分解、剪枝；它们要么只能减少每个参数的字节数，要么依赖离线近似，难以根据当前输入动态决定哪些权重读取其实是多余的。论文的核心判断是：在固定层上，真实激活并不会铺满整个 d 维空间，而是集中在一个低维子空间里；因此，对当前输入分布真正有用的，不是完整权重矩阵 W，而是 W 在该子空间上的像 WV_k。问题就变成：能否在线跟踪这个子空间，并在不明显改变输出分布的前提下，只读取与该子空间相关的低秩权重像，从而减少带宽读写。","importance":"对基座研究的直接意义不在预训练算法，而在于它把“激活流形低维”这个表征性质，转成了可执行的推理系统设计。这个角度比静态权重压缩更接近模型真实运行时的输入依赖结构。若这个假设在更大模型和更长上下文下持续成立，它提示了一个重要方向：模型的有效推理参数量可能远小于总参数量，而且这个“有效参数量”是随输入变化的。对预训练研究员来说，这有两层启发。第一，训练时是否可以显式塑造更稳定、更低维的层内表示，从而让后续推理更容易做动态低秩化。第二，评估模型容量时，不能只看参数总数，还要看不同输入分布下的有效激活维度。贡献量级上，这篇更像一篇扎实的 inference-side 方法论文，而不是改变训练范式的工作；但它把 activation subspace、动态门控、带宽 roofline 三件事连在了一起，思路是清楚的。","technical_approach":"方法名是 Gated Subspace Inference，基本机制可以拆成三步。第一步，在每一层维护一个在线更新的正交基 V_k^(l)，用于近似该层当前 token 激活所在的 k 维子空间。论文沿用 SSI 中的 DGKS rank-1 更新，在时间维上逐 token 扩展或修正这个基，而不是预先离线估计一个固定 PCA 子空间。这样做的原因是解码过程中的激活分布会随上下文变化，静态子空间容易失配。\n\n第二步，对该层所有线性映射共享同一个子空间基，包括 attention 的 Q/K/V 投影、输出投影，以及 MLP 的 up/down 投影。对任意线性层 y=Wx，先把输入分解成 x=V_kg+r，其中 g=V_k^T x，r 是残差。然后预缓存低秩权重像 M=WV_k。若只走快路径，则输出近似为 y≈Mg，只需读取 M 而不是完整 W，带宽从 d_out×d 降到 d_out×k。相比 SSI 只覆盖 MLP 两个投影，GSI 把 QKV 和 O 也纳入，覆盖了更大比例的线性层带宽开销。\n\n第三步，用一个逐 token 的门控决定是否需要计算残差修正 Wr。门控信号来自残差相对范数 ρ_t=||r_t||/||x_t||。当 ρ_t 足够小，说明当前激活几乎落在子空间内，只用 Mg 就够；当 ρ_t 超过阈值，再回退到慢路径，计算完整 Wx 或至少补上 Wr。论文强调这个 gate 不是为了做启发式稀疏，而是为了把输出分布误差控制在给定容忍范围内。也就是说，它试图在“近似计算”和“分布保持”之间给出一个可调旋钮。\n\n和已有方法相比，差异主要有三点。其一，不做静态权重低秩分解，而是做输入相关的 activation-conditioned 低秩化。其二，不是神经元级或元素级 sparsity，而是连续子空间上的压缩。其三，不是无条件近似，而是加了 residual-aware gate，允许在难 token 上退回精确计算。这个设计比纯低秩近似更稳，也更适合语言模型里 token 难度分布很不均匀的场景。","experiments":"论文在三类模型上做了验证：GPT-2 124M、GPT-J 6B、OPT 6.7B，硬件平台是 AMD MI300X。摘要给出的核心结果是线性层权重读取的有效加速达到 3.0× 到 10.5×，同时 perplexity ratio 低于 1.00，top-k 指标基本保持。这里要注意，作者强调的是 linear-layer weight reads 的加速，不等于端到端 wall-clock 一定同倍率提升；因为 attention、kernel launch、缓存管理和门控本身也有开销。就系统论文的口径看，这个表述是谨慎的。\n\n从全文可读出的关键实验重点有三类。第一类是子空间维度 k 与质量/加速的权衡。k 越小，缓存的 M=WV_k 越省带宽，但残差越大，触发慢路径的 token 越多；因此真实收益取决于“低秩近似收益”和“回退频率”之间的平衡。第二类是 gate 阈值的敏感性。阈值放宽会提高快路径命中率，但误差也会上升；阈值收紧则更接近无损推理，但加速下降。第三类是层覆盖范围的贡献。把方法从 SSI 的 MLP-only 扩展到 full transformer 后，收益明显增加，因为 QKV 和输出投影本身就占了大约一半线性层读流量。\n\n摘要里没有给出更细的逐模型 perplexity、top-k 保真率、不同 k 的 ablation 数字；如果全文表格中有这些值，阅读时应重点核对两件事：一是质量保持是否在长文本和分布外 prompt 上也稳定；二是 10.5× 这类高倍数是否只出现在较小 k、较宽松 gate 或特定层/特定模型上。对这类方法，平均值往往不如尾部行为重要，因为少量高残差 token 就可能触发频繁回退，拉低真实吞吐。","reading_guide":"泛读建议是先看 Introduction 里对 roofline 和带宽瓶颈的界定，再看方法部分关于 x=V_kg+r、M=WV_k 和 residual gate 的推导。这几页决定了方法是否成立。随后重点看三类图表：一是各层激活有效秩或 explained variance 的统计，确认低维假设是否足够强；二是 gate 触发率、残差范数分布与 perplexity 之间的关系，判断“近似何时安全”；三是端到端或至少模块级带宽/时延拆解，确认收益主要来自哪里。\n\n预计阅读时间 20 到 30 分钟。若只想抓住要点，看方法总览图、子空间更新算法、以及主结果表即可。若想判断是否值得继续跟进，要深挖两个问题：第一，共享一个 layer-wise basis 给 QKV、O、MLP 是否过于粗糙，不同投影是否其实需要不同子空间；第二，在线更新 basis 的数值稳定性和缓存维护成本，在更大模型、更长上下文、以及 speculative decoding 场景下是否仍然划算。","authors_note":""},"summary_en":{"core_problem":"The paper targets a fundamental bottleneck in batch-1 autoregressive decoding: transformer inference is often limited by memory bandwidth rather than compute. At each generated token, the model repeatedly reads large weight matrices from HBM, and the linear-layer traffic scales roughly as O(Ld^2). Prior acceleration methods mostly operate on the weight side—quantization, static low-rank factorization, pruning—but they either reduce bytes per parameter without reducing the number of reads, or rely on offline approximations that do not adapt to the current input. The paper’s key observation is that layer activations for the current sequence occupy a low-dimensional subspace of the hidden space. If so, the useful part of a weight matrix W is not the full matrix, but its image on that subspace, WV_k. The practical question becomes whether one can track that subspace online and use it to avoid unnecessary weight reads while keeping the output distribution nearly unchanged.","importance":"$d","technical_approach":"$e","experiments":"$f","reading_guide":"$10","authors_note":""},"recommendation":{"zh":"无重训推理加速的有效方案，推理框架研发人员可参考门控设计部分。","en":"Effective training-free inference acceleration scheme, inference framework researchers can refer to the gating design section."},"metadata":{"institutions":["Unspecified University"],"authors":["Stephen J. 框架上加入 text-conditional predictor，形成 TC-JEPA。基础结构仍是 teacher-student 式 JEPA：在线编码器提取上下文 patch 表征，EMA target encoder 提供被遮挡 patch 的目标特征，predictor 根据上下文和 mask token 预测目标 patch 表征。\n\n核心改动在 predictor graph tokens，再作为前缀喂给 LLM，模型到底有没有在自然语言嵌入空间里“理解”这些 token？现有 GTokenLLM 工作通常默认这种做法比把图转成文本描述更有效，也更像通用图基础模型的方向；但这些 graph tokens 来自外部图编码器和投影器，不对应词表中的任何自然语言单位，语义是否真正被 LLM 吸收，其实并不清楚。论文不是再提一个新的 graph-LLM
